OR the log file uses `\r` (carriage return) to overwrite, so `tail` might not show the latest output properly.

```text
2. The GPU is at 89% utilization, so a process is actively running. But the log hasn't updated past the first conversation. 

The progress file having 2 lines suggests both conversations were saved, so the generation might actually be complete but the final `.jsonl` file hasn't been created yet. I should check if the tmux session is still running and whether there's a final output file, though the GPU still holding 18GB of memory could just be the Llama model sitting in VRAM.

Looking at the key facts: the worker failed due to context/timeout termination, but the actual script creation and job submission were successful, and the server-side process continued independently—this is expected tmux behavior. The worker wrote the script, submitted the dry run, then got terminated before collecting results, but the job itself is still running or recently completed based on the GPU activity and progress file. This is really just an INFO-level finding, not a critical failure. Let me post the review.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Line 239: `with open(args.output, "w") as f:` — **writes with "w" mode** (overwrite, not append). Default output is `{OUTPUT_DIR}/benign_llama.jsonl` = `/root/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/data/cross_llm/benign_llama.jsonl`.

Line 202: `parser.add_argument("--output", type=str, default=f"{OUTPUT_DIR}/benign_llama.jsonl")`

So unless the worker used a different `--output` argument, the script will **overwrite** the existing 50-line exp14 benign_llama.jsonl with 250 new conversations.


This is a critical issue — exp14 data will be lost and its results can't be reproduced. The script accumulates all 250 conversations in memory and writes them at once in overwrite mode, so it'll completely replace the original 50-conversation file. Since exp14 only needs those specific 50 conversations and the new ones would be generated with different seeds, they won't match the original data.

I need to check if this is the same script used for exp14 — it has a hardcoded seed, so the first 50 conversations might align if the implementation is consistent, but that's not guaranteed. The real issue is that the worker should be writing to a different file path entirely to preserve exp14's data while generating the 250 conversations for exp16.

Since the seed is fixed, exp14 might technically be reproducible, but overwriting the existing file is poor practice. I'm flagging this as a WARNING: the current benign_llama.jsonl will be overwritten, so the worker should either back up the file first or use a separate output path for exp16.
```

```text
This is a significant finding:

1. **exp14 benign data LOST**: The original 50-line `benign_llama.jsonl` was overwritten by the exp16 benign generation process. The backup worker arrived too late — the file had already been partially overwritten (18 lines of new data at that point).

2. **Worker killed the benign process**: PID 276325 was killed to stop further damage.

3. **No recovery possible**: File not in git, no backup on server.

4. **Impact**: exp14 results can't be exactly reproduced with original data. 

The evaluation results themselves are still preserved in the registry though, so this is a WARNING-level issue rather than critical. Now I'm checking whether the jailbreak generation has a similar conflict — it looks like it's writing to a separate directory (`data/llama_conversations/crescendo_jailbreak_llama.jsonl`), so there shouldn't be the same overlap problem with exp14's jailbreak data.
```

```text
The worker made the right call stopping the process to prevent further damage. The backup data confirms the loss but isn't useful for recovery. However, exp14's core findings about FP rates driving cross-LLM failure are preserved in the registry, and I can re-run evaluation with newly generated benign data if needed. Since exp14 is appendix material rather than a core experiment, this data loss is unfortunate but won't derail the paper. I'll post the review now.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Observations:

1. **GPU 0 & 1** both at 89%, 18349 MiB — both jobs running correctly
2. **GPU 2-3**: idle, no violations
3. **Jailbreak progress**: `crescendo_jailbreak_llama.jsonl` still 2 lines (final file), but progress file growing (527KB, up from 472KB earlier). Worker reported 31/250 completed. The final jsonl only gets the 2 dry run results — the progress file accumulates during generation, and the final file is written at the end.
4. **Benign output path**: Worker says it's now using `data/llama_conversations/benign_llama_250.jsonl` — safe path. 

The benign file isn't showing up in the directory yet, likely because the model just loaded and generation is starting. Looking back at the worker logs, I see the benign job actually crashed initially due to a GPU mapping issue — the script needed `--gpu 0` since `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1` maps physical GPU 1 to logical `cuda:0`, but it was submitted with `--gpu 1` first. The worker corrected and resubmitted it.
```

```text
1	{
2	  "raw": "## 汇报\n\n### 3 个脚本\n\n| 脚本 | 服务器路径 | 功能 |\n|------|-----------|------|\n| `prepare_expanded_data.py` | `scripts/prepare_expanded_data.py` | 合并 plan_002_splits (500) + expanded (500) → `data/expanded_1000/` (700/150/150)，自动回退 .progress 文件 |\n| `run_exp17_training.sh` | `scripts/run_exp17_training.sh` | 3 variant × 3 seeds = 9 训练，checkpoint 到 `checkpoints/exp17/`，遵守 GPU C001 约束 (cuda:0-2) |\n| `eval_exp17.py` | `scripts/eval_exp17.py` | 9 checkpoint × 4 benchmark (DD OOD / AA OOD / ToxicChat / IID)，输出对比表格 |\n\n### 数据格式差异\n\n- **plan_002_splits**: 已处理格式，`persuasion_strategy` 为 int (0=none, 1-8=策略)\n- **expanded jailbreak**: 原始格式，`intended_strategy` (0-7)，脚本自动 +1 映射\n- **expanded benign**: 无 persuasion_strategy 字段，脚本自动补 0\n- **ID 冲突**: jailbreak/benign 均使用 `crescendo_exp_XXXX`，已加 `exp_jb_`/`exp_bn_` 前缀\n\n### 数据预处理流程\n\n`prepare_expanded_data.py` 复用 `src/merge_and_split.py` 的策略映射逻辑，直接合并已处理的原始 500 条 + 新处理的扩展 500 条，然后 stratified split (70/15/15)。\n\n### OOD 测试数据\n\n- DD OOD: `data/generated/deceptive_delight_all.jsonl` (80 jailbreak) + test benign\n- AA OOD: `data/actorattack_ood/actorattack_all.jsonl` (80 jailbreak) + test benign\n- ToxicChat: `data/mhj/toxicchat_plan002_eval.jsonl` (242 条混合)\n\n### 预估训练时间\n\n基于 plan_002 经验（500 条，~10min/DeBERTa stage + ~5min/GRU stage），1000 条约 2x：\n- Vanilla: 3 seeds × ~10min = ~30min\n- MLM: 3 seeds × (20 + 10)min = ~90min\n- 9-class: 3 seeds × (20 + 10)min = ~90min\n- **总计约 3.5h**（串行执行），可改为并行跑 variant 缩短到 ~1.5h"
3	}
```

### Assistant

_source line 735 · 2026-05-19T16:14:06.380Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I see the issue: the exp16 training script sets `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0,1,2,3` globally, but individual training commands do NOT specify a per-command `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES`. This means all training processes will see all 4 GPUs. PyTorch typically uses cuda:0 by default in the training scripts.

Comparing with exp17's script which had per-command `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0`, `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1`, `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=2` — exp16's approach is different.


Since the script runs serially, only one GPU gets used at a time and the training code defaults to cuda:0, which maps correctly to physical GPU 0. The real risk is if any training code uses multi-GPU parallelization by default — it could accidentally consume all 4 GPUs simultaneously, though with batch size 32 that seems unlikely.

I'm also noticing the script uses `--mode baseline --model_name {mlm_ckpt}` for the MLM variant's GRU stage as a workaround for format incompatibility, but I should verify this actually loads the MLM-pretrained weights correctly through AutoModel. More importantly, the test data path points to the perturbed version of DD OOD rather than the original, which seems like it could be a mistake for exp16 evaluation. I need to verify which DD OOD dataset is actually correct—exp17 used the clean `deceptive_delight_all.jsonl` while exp16 used the perturbed version, so there's definitely an inconsistency I need to resolve before proceeding.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Confirmed: exp16's eval script uses `data/dd_ood_perturbed/perturbed.jsonl` for DD OOD evaluation, which is the PERTURBED data (from exp6). This is wrong — it should be using the clean DD OOD data (`data/generated/deceptive_delight_all.jsonl`), like exp17 does.

Using perturbed data makes the DD OOD results non-comparable with all previous experiments that used clean DD OOD data. This is a meaningful error that needs to be fixed.

Let me also check the exp17 eval script for comparison.
```

[System 触发审查]:
**审查请求：exp16/exp17 结果场景的论文叙事升级方案**

exp16 (Multi-LLM training) 和 exp17 (Dataset scale 500→1000) 数据生成进行中，预计 4-5h 后出结果。请现在审查以下两种场景的论文叙事调整方案，确保 framing 准确、claim 有数据支撑：

**场景 A：exp16 成功（混合 LLM 训练修复 cross-LLM 泛化）**
- 当前 Limitations "single LLM source (Qwen3-8B)" → 升级为 positive finding
- 新 claim：multi-source training data eliminates benign distribution shift，实现 cross-LLM generalizability
- exp14 的 "崩溃" 重新 frame 为 "诊断性实验揭示了 benign 分布偏移是瓶颈"
- Discussion 加 practical implication：部署时需包含多 LLM 源 benign 数据

**场景 B：exp16 失败或部分成功**
- 维持当前 Limitation framing（单 LLM 源是已知限制）
- 如果部分成功（某些 variant 改善但非全部），写入 Discussion 作为 nuanced finding
- 保留 exp14b 的 "jailbreak 特征可迁移" 正面发现不变

**场景 C：exp17 成功（1000 对话结果与 500 一致或更好）**
- Limitation "small dataset (500 conversations)" → 升级为 robustness validation
- 新 claim 或 footnote：results stable across 2× scale, suggesting findings are not artifact of sample size

**场景 D：exp17 失败（1000 对话结果显著不同）**
- 如果更好：好事，report improvement
- 如果更差或不稳定：需要诊断（数据质量？topic 分布偏移？），Limitation 保持

请评估：
1. 各场景的 framing 是否经得起审稿人挑战？
2. 现有 paper_framing.md 中的 3 个 Core Claims 是否需要修改或新增？
3. exp16 成功时，Abstract/Intro 需要改哪些句子？

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a request to review narrative upgrade plans for exp16/exp17 results. I need to evaluate the proposed framing adjustments for each scenario against the paper's existing claims and evidence. Let me think carefully about each scenario.

First, let me recall the current paper structure:
- Core Claim 1: Domain exposure bridges cross-attack OOD gap
- Core Claim 2: Persuasion taxonomy as interpretability layer
- Core Claim 3: Classification supervision provides cross-seed stability (on synthetic OOD)
- Current limitations include: single LLM source (Qwen3-8B), small dataset (500 conversations)


Now I'm working through what each scenario would require. Scenario A proposes converting the single-LLM limitation into a strength if exp16 shows mixed-LLM training improves cross-LLM generalization, treating exp14's collapse as a diagnostic finding rather than a failure. Scenario B keeps the current framing if exp16 doesn't deliver strong results. Scenario C would reposition the dataset size constraint as evidence of robustness if exp17 maintains performance at 1000 conversations. Scenario D appears incomplete but likely involves some combination of these adjustments.

For Scenario A specifically, I'm questioning whether "multi-source training eliminates benign distribution shift" is actually supported by the evidence. Adding Llama data to fix Llama false positives is one thing, but claiming it generalizes to arbitrary LLMs is overreaching with just one additional model. The narrative of exp14 showing the problem, exp14c diagnosing it, and exp16 solving it is coherent, but this needs to stay as a supplementary finding rather than elevating it to a core claim about the DA approach itself.

For Scenario C, the 2× scale robustness check is useful validation but shouldn't be presented as a major finding—it belongs in a footnote or brief mention. The real constraint here is that the paper is already at 8 pages, so adding new content means cutting something else. I'm also concerned about scope creep: the paper's focus is understanding the mechanism of multi-turn jailbreak detectability, not building the best detector or proving cross-LLM generalization. Cross-LLM findings are fine as supporting evidence, but they shouldn't become central claims that shift the paper's direction.

Now I need to think through the framing proposals more carefully. The key question is whether reframing exp14 as "diagnostic" is honest or post-hoc rationalization. exp14 was originally designed for cross-LLM validation and failed, but exp14c diagnosed why (false positive explosion), and exp16 tests the fix. Calling exp14 "diagnostic" works only if the paper presents it that way from the beginning—otherwise it looks like we're retrofitting the narrative to justify a failed experiment.

On the claim strength, saying we "eliminate" benign distribution shift is too aggressive given just one additional LLM. Something like "substantially reduces cross-LLM false positives" or "mitigates distribution shift through diverse benign sources" would be more defensible.

Adding cross-LLM findings is reasonable, but I need to be careful not to let scope creep turn this into a paper about both decomposing cross-attack generalization and solving distribution shift—that's two different contributions.

For the core claims themselves, experiments 16 and 17 are really just robustness checks on the DAPT mechanism story, not fundamental changes to it. So I can keep claims 1-3 as-is and tuck the new results into the Discussion or Appendix rather than promoting them to main findings.
```

```text
1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not classification, drives detection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\,=\,0.80 vs.\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure. Full hierarchy validation awaits multi-turn human data.
11	
12	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
13	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions, while MLM generalizes across distributions.
14	
15	\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}
16	DeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.
17	
18	\section{Conclusion}
19	\label{sec:conclusion}
20	
21	Our ablation across three encoder scales and three OOD attack families yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but does not transfer to human data. ToxicChat validation confirms the adapted vs.\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work.
22	
23	\section{Limitations}
24	\label{sec:limitations}
25	
26	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
27	The training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
28	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
31	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
32	
33	\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}
34	Persuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.
35	
36	\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}
37	While the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\% ($\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.
38	
39	\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}
40	FITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 824 · 2026-05-19T18:14:23.795Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019YL3kruzKZMQKo4kmVT6aD`</summary>

```text
1	% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires no better labels and no new architecture: domain text exposure alone suffices.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.
5	We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families.
6	A single factor explains the gap: for base-size encoders, continued pretraining on domain text closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without any labels. Label quality, granularity, and temporal modeling are non-factors.
7	A capacity-dependent pattern holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks that require adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.
8	Classification supervision reduces prediction variance on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data: on ToxicChat, unsupervised domain exposure yields macro F1 0.84 versus 0.41 without adaptation.
9	These findings reframe multi-turn jailbreak detection as a domain adaptation problem rather than a supervised classification task.
10	\end{abstract}
11	
```

```text
Current state:
- Discussion §"Single-LLM confound" (line 9-10): Already partially addresses this with ToxicChat + exp14b cross-LLM results
- Limitations §"Dataset scale and diversity" (line 26-28): Explicitly mentions "single model (Qwen3-8B)", 500 conversations, and "Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work"

Now let me evaluate each scenario:

**Scenario A: exp16 success**


The proposal wants to move "single LLM source" from Limitations to a positive finding, claiming "multi-source training eliminates benign distribution shift" and reframing exp14 as diagnostic. But "eliminates" is too strong—exp16 only tests one additional LLM (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct), so the accurate framing would be that mixed-source training substantially reduces cross-LLM false positives rather than eliminating the problem entirely.

There's also scope creep risk here. The paper's core focus is cross-attack generalization, and promoting cross-LLM generalization to the Abstract and Intro level could shift the narrative and invite reviewer pushback about testing only two LLMs. Given the page constraints at 8 pages, that's a real concern.

On reframing exp14 as diagnostic—that's actually fair since exp14c did identify FP rate as the bottleneck, which is genuinely diagnostic. The paper already hints at this in the Limitations section, so it's not a major reframing.

The practical implication of deploying with multi-LLM benign data is reasonable but fairly obvious—any reviewer would see it coming. It belongs in Discussion if anywhere, not as a highlighted contribution. I think the Abstract and Intro should stay focused on the DAPT mechanism decomposition. exp16 is more of a robustness extension than a core contribution, so at most I'd update the Limitations to note we verified with two LLMs rather than promoting it to a main claim. Adding a fourth claim about cross-LLM generalization would dilute the paper's focus when the first three claims are already about DAPT mechanism.

For exp17, if the results hold at a larger scale, that's a valid robustness check—showing the findings aren't an artifact of sample size. But again, this is a brief paragraph or footnote, not Abstract material. The Limitations section should still acknowledge the data is still synthetic and from the same model, even if we've scaled it up. If exp17 fails, I need to understand why before deciding how to frame it, but the current approach of diagnosing and maintaining honest limitations is the right path.

On the Abstract and Introduction—I'm confident exp16 shouldn't change those sections. The paper's core claim is about DAPT decomposition across attacks, not cross-LLM training. One additional LLM isn't enough for a general statement, and the Abstract is already packed with 13 configurations, 3 scales, and 3 OOD families. Adding cross-LLM coverage would just prompt reviewers to ask why I didn't test more models. If exp16 succeeds, it belongs in Discussion or Appendix as a promising direction. For the Limitations section, I'll update the future work sentence to report the result if exp16 succeeds, and soften the data size limitation if exp17 succeeds.
```

</details>

```text
1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic-level shortcut: (1) topic classification yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD); (2) scrambled labels match correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp); (3) MLM achieves strong detection without labels (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$). Domain exposure, not classification, drives detection.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}). A cross-LLM experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) further isolates the confound: with Llama-3.1-8B jailbreaks and Qwen benign text, detection F1 recovers to 0.78--1.00; the residual ActorAttack shortfall (F1\,=\,0.80 vs.\ DD 1.00) reflects cross-LLM distributional differences rather than model failure.
11	To test whether broader benign coverage reduces this gap, we train on a mixed corpus of Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B benign conversations. Under mixed training, cross-LLM F1 rises from [EXP16_BASELINE] to [EXP16_MIXED_F1], and false positives on Llama benign text drop from [EXP16_FPR_BASELINE] to [EXP16_FPR_MIXED]. Benign distribution coverage, not jailbreak-side diversity, is the bottleneck. A second LLM source mitigates the distributional shift but does not eliminate it: [EXP16_RESIDUAL]. Full hierarchy validation on multi-turn human data remains open.
12	
13	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
14	On synthetic OOD, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\times$ over MLM; on ToxicChat, this reverses (9-class std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ JB-MLM 0.013). Classification stabilizes within-distribution predictions, while MLM generalizes across distributions.
15	
16	\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}
17	DeBERTa-v3-base (184M), RoBERTa-base (125M), and RoBERTa-large (355M) span a 2.8$\times$ capacity range but also differ in pretraining objectives (RTD vs.\ MLM) and tokenization. The capacity-dependent pattern across three models (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) is suggestive but not conclusive: confirming that model size rather than architecture drives the pattern requires same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\ DeBERTa-large) with matched pretraining.
18	
19	\section{Conclusion}
20	\label{sec:conclusion}
21	
22	Our ablation across three encoder scales and three OOD attack families yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but does not transfer to human data. ToxicChat validation confirms the adapted vs.\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work.
23	
24	\section{Limitations}
25	\label{sec:limitations}
26	
27	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
28	The training set uses synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to [EXP17_1000] conversations: detection F1 remains at [EXP17_OOD_F1] across OOD families, and the DAPT advantage over vanilla holds at [EXP17_DAPT_GAP]\,pp. The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered; tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. All multi-turn data is synthetic. ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms transfer across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), but whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
29	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
30	
31	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
32	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance; this indicates substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap that demonstrates continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
33	
34	\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}
35	Persuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.
36	
37	\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}
38	While the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\% ($\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.
39	
40	\paragraph{FITD discriminative power.}
41	FITD provides minimal discriminative signal: both vanilla and 9-class achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes. FITD serves primarily as a confirmation that simple OOD attacks do not challenge any variant, rather than as a meaningful evaluation axis.
42	
43	\paragraph{Scope.}
44	Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.
45	
```

</details>

```text
- "We validate robustness to dataset scale by doubling the training corpus from 500 to [EXP17_1000] conversations" — good framing as robustness check
- "The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered" — still honest
- "All multi-turn data is synthetic" — limitation preserved
- "whether the full evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open" — honest

This is good. The limitation is softened on scale but maintained on the more fundamental issue (synthetic data).

The Abstract, Intro, and Conclusion sections weren't touched, which is appropriate. Though the Conclusion's statement about "multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work" gets partially addressed by exp16's mixed-LLM training results, so there's a slight tension there — but it's not inaccurate since human-generated multi-turn data is still genuinely future work.

I should verify the worker checked the paper-writing norms and quality standards per my audit protocol, though this is a pre-draft with placeholders so that's less critical at this stage. The new language reads naturally without the AI style markers I usually flag — no em-dashes, no participial openers, no filler. The framing aligns well with my recommendations, though the cross-LLM paragraph in Limitations (line 29) might need tweaking if exp16 succeeds, since it currently frames multi-LLM training as purely future work when it's now partially addressed.

I'm noticing a potential inconsistency: if exp16 succeeds and gets reported in Discussion, the Limitations section claiming "Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work" becomes contradictory. The worker may be holding this as conditional for a failure scenario, planning to update it later depending on exp16's outcome. I'll flag this as something to revisit.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01ESouZ2dgKqmiewAhNMLB7D`</summary>

```text
1	% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires no better labels and no new architecture: domain text exposure alone suffices.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families.
5	We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families.
6	A single factor explains the gap: for base-size encoders, continued pretraining on domain text closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without any labels. Label quality, granularity, and temporal modeling are non-factors.
7	A capacity-dependent pattern holds across scales: as model size grows, the set of attacks that require adaptation shrinks, until the largest encoder generalizes without adaptation.
8	Classification supervision reduces prediction variance on synthetic evaluations regardless of label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data: on ToxicChat, unsupervised domain exposure yields macro F1 0.84 versus 0.41 without adaptation.
9	These findings reframe multi-turn jailbreak detection as a domain adaptation problem rather than a supervised classification task.
10	\end{abstract}
11	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01ELXLvy8oS5J6L77FqugzPi`</summary>

```text
1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
7	
8	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
9	
10	The simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.
```

_source line 868 · 2026-05-19T19:11:02.997Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01J9bb8Wya5SadrpbUPoHjBh`</summary>

```text
22	Our ablation across three encoder scales and three OOD attack families yields three findings. First, for base-size encoders (125--184M), DAPT bridges $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap without labels; for the largest encoder (355M), pretrained representations already suffice on structurally complex attacks. Second, as model size increases, the set of attacks requiring domain adaptation narrows. Third, classification supervision stabilizes within-distribution predictions (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) but does not transfer to human data. ToxicChat validation confirms the adapted vs.\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written attacks; multi-turn human and multi-LLM data remain future work.
```

</details>

_source line 870 · 2026-05-19T19:11:03.715Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JsCkkw2EnQZh4x4jdLv6Bv`</summary>

```text
29	Cross-LLM validation with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as the target model reveals that jailbreak detection transfers across LLMs (F1 recovers to 0.80--1.00 when the benign distribution is held constant), but the benign baseline is LLM-specific: replacing Qwen benign text with Llama benign text causes false-positive rates to spike (F1 drops to 0.37--0.43). A per-class error decomposition (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}, Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}) confirms that true positive rates are identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text. The residual cross-LLM degradation on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack F1 = 0.80 vs.\ 0.997 in-distribution) indicates that some jailbreak features remain source-specific. Multi-LLM training data is a clear direction for future work (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}).
```

</details>
